\paragraph{Services that should not be used.}
Two observations follow from the sources; they are not decisions. The
Gemini API's terms forbid using it in a website or app that is directed
towards, or likely to be accessed by, people under 18, so Squigglr could
not use it as a refinement provider under those terms. The same terms let
Google use content sent to the free tier to improve its products, with
human review \citep{Google2026GeminiTerms}; any free tier with such terms
would expose
children's drawings in a way the supervisor's advice (SRC-004) and the
open privacy issue (OI-02) argue against. A service that returns only a
bitmap image does not meet the need for editable strokes (SRC-002).

\subsection{Products That Can Be Copied}
\label{sec:ots-copy}
Volere asks this question of every project and warns that copying relies
on the base being of good quality \citep{robertson2012volere}. The
team has not evaluated the quality of any base listed here and found no
published specification of any surveyed product that could be reused.
The answer therefore has three parts: code and data that may legally be
copied, interaction patterns worth imitating, and patterns to avoid.

\paragraph{Code and data whose licences allow copying.}
Paper.js and Simplify.js allow reuse as long as their copyright notices
are kept \citep{PaperJS2020License,Agafonkin2013SimplifyJS}, and the
Magenta code is under the Apache License 2.0 \citep{Magenta2016License}.
Tux Paint's source code may be copied under the GPL version~2
\citep{TuxPaint2026Download}, whose conditions would then apply to the
copied code; Tux Paint is built as an installed program, not a website.
The Quick, Draw! drawings may be copied under the CC BY 4.0 licence, but
they were contributed by players
of a public game, the dataset records no player ages, and the drawings
may still contain inappropriate content \citep{Google2017QuickDraw}. They
therefore cannot serve as the adult-made drawings from which Squigglr's
ranges are set (BR-009, ASM-014).

\paragraph{Interaction patterns worth imitating.}
Ideas, not code, are the most useful things to copy.
\begin{itemize}
  \item LetterSchool's tap, trace and write sequence gives the same item
  with less help each time \citep{LetterSchool2026HowWhy}, as Squigglr's
  stages do (G.1, G.2).
  \item Tracing Playground marks the start with a dot, shows direction
  with an arrow, numbers the strokes, says in words when the finger leaves
  the path, has no timer or score, and follows the device's reduced-motion
  setting \citep{TracingTown2026Playground}; these suit children who may
  not read (FACT-007).
  \item Writing Wizard's and Kaligo's replays of what the child traced
  \citep{Abel2026WritingWizard,LearnAndGo2026Teachers} show adults the
  actual strokes behind a result (G.5, BR-004).
  \item QuickShape keeps a corrected shape editable
  \citep{Procreate2026QuickShape}, and Smart Script keeps the look of the
  writer's own hand \citep{Apple2024SmartScript}; both match the intent
  of BR-005.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Patterns to avoid.}
Skill screening, scores against a threshold and comparison with reference
ranges conflict with BR-006, and replacing the child's drawing with an
artist's conflicts with BR-005. No surveyed product can be copied whole,
because none combines the dimensions in Table~\ref{tab:ots-dimensions}.

\section{New Problems}
\label{sec:new-problems}
This section records the problems that introducing Squigglr may create:
in the places where it is used, for the systems it runs alongside, for its
users, in the environment it is built for, and later in its life. Each
problem is stated as a cause and its effect, followed by the precaution
this specification already takes or the open item that will decide it.
Links point to business rules (BR) and assumptions (ASM) in
Section~\ref{sec:facts-assumptions}, open issues (OI) in
Table~\ref{tab:openIssues} and likely changes (LC) in
Table~\ref{tab:likelyChanges}. Critical assumptions (CA) and hazard
identifiers (H-RAY, H-AHM, H-MUH) from the Hazard Analysis, and the safety
requirements (SAF-RAY, SAF-AHM) in Safety-Critical Requirements, are given
as plain text.
Nothing in this section is a requirement; each precaution names where the
related requirement or decision is, or will be, recorded.

\subsection{Effects on the Current Environment}
Squigglr will be used at home, in classrooms and in places such as
rehabilitation centres (Anticipated Workplace Environment).
Table~\ref{tab:new-problems-environment} lists how it changes the work of
the adults and children in those places. The Volere template also asks
what the product must not do there; the last row answers that.

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.39\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.35\textwidth}@{}}
\caption{New problems: effects on the current environment}
\label{tab:new-problems-environment}\\
\toprule
\textbf{Problem} & \textbf{Cause and effect} &
\textbf{Precaution and links} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Problem} & \textbf{Cause and effect} &
\textbf{Precaution and links} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot
More work for supporting adults &
Squigglr gives adults new tasks: creating a learner profile, choosing six
adult-set values, with example drawings to help with
\texttt{STAGE\_PASS} (BR-008), choosing lessons and starting stages,
answering go-back suggestions (BR-003) and reviewing records. An educator
with a short slot per learner (PER-3) or a
parent who reviews once a week (PER-2) may leave defaults in place or let
suggestions wait. &
Each value defaults to the middle of its range (BR-008); a declined or
ignored suggestion changes nothing (G.3); the user documentation in
Section~\ref{sec:user-docs} explains each task. Links: ASM-016, CA-006;
SAF-RAY-001, SAF-RAY-008; H-RAY-001, H-RAY-007. \\
\addlinespace
Shared and centrally managed school devices &
One school device may be used by several children (SRC-004), and
educators cannot install software on managed devices (PER-3). A session
left open, or a learner not switched, can show one child's records to
another child or save attempts to the wrong learner profile. &
An adult selects the right learner (ASM-008); how a shared device is
signed in and left after a session is OI-01; SAF-RAY-007 identifies the
active learner. Hazards: H-RAY-006, H-MUH-004. \\
\addlinespace
Practice moves onto a screen &
Practice with Squigglr is time on a device. Squigglr cannot see how long a
child has used the device, or whether the child is distressed or
uncomfortable (ASM-002), so long or repeated sessions can go unnoticed
when no adult is watching. &
The adult stays responsible for the child's time on the device (ASM-002)
and can stop a session or mute audio (ASM-015); a lesson ends at
\texttt{ATTEMPT\_LIMIT}, or at \texttt{ATTEMPT\_LIMIT\_MAX} when extended
(G.3). Hazards: H-RAY-004, H-RAY-005. \\
\addlinespace
Relationship with professional support &
Occupational therapists and educators may already support the child.
Squigglr keeps practice records, but the specialists advised deciding with
such professionals which measures help an adult
(SRC-005, \intref{P2}{31:42}). An adult could treat Squigglr as a
substitute for that support. &
Squigglr makes no claim that its practice improves any skill (BR-006;
FACT-001, FACT-002); the user documentation states that Squigglr offers
practice, not therapy or assessment. \\
\addlinespace
What Squigglr must not do &
Squigglr must not take over decisions that belong to the adults around the
child. Squigglr never selects another lesson, skips a stage or moves a
child back a stage without an adult's authorization (CON-007, BR-003),
never gives a child personalized AI-generated feedback in a guided
activity (BR-007), never replaces a child's strokes in a guided-lesson
record (BR-004), and never lets refinement act outside the creative space
(BR-005). Breaking
any of these would override an adult's judgement or mislead the adults who
rely on the records. &
These are business rules and unlikely changes (ULC-003 to ULC-005,
Table~\ref{tab:unlikelyChanges}). Hazards: H-MUH-002 (refinement reaching
a guided-lesson record), H-MUH-003 (assisted work shown as
independent). \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsection{Effects on the Installed Systems}
Squigglr installs nothing on the user's device (CON-002) and replaces no
existing system (Implementation Environment of the Current System). The
systems it must coexist with are the browsers, devices and networks that
homes and schools already use, and the outside services Squigglr itself
depends on. Table~\ref{tab:new-problems-installed} lists the conflicts
that can arise with each.

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.39\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.35\textwidth}@{}}
\caption{New problems: effects on the installed systems}
\label{tab:new-problems-installed}\\
\toprule
\textbf{System} & \textbf{Cause and effect} &
\textbf{Precaution and links} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{System} & \textbf{Cause and effect} &
\textbf{Precaution and links} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot
Browsers and devices already in use &
Squigglr runs in whatever browser the adult's or school's device has
(CON-001). Squigglr depends on that browser reporting stylus strokes in
enough detail (ASM-004), on devices the team does not control (CA-002),
and on browser updates not changing how input is reported. Strokes may
then be captured wrongly, or not at all, on some devices. &
The supported device and browser combinations are OI-09; SAF-RAY-002 shows
a notice on an unsupported device; drawing input can be read through one
input module (LC-003). Hazard: H-RAY-002. \\
\addlinespace
Browser storage on a shared device &
Keeping each completed attempt on the device until the server confirms
that it is saved (SAF-RAY-003) means practice data may sit in the browser
of a shared or school device. The next user of the device could see or
overwrite another child's unsaved attempts, or the data could stay on the
device after the session ends. &
How a shared device is left after a session is OI-01; who may see records
is part of the privacy requirements (OI-02). Hazards: H-RAY-006,
H-MUH-004. \\
\addlinespace
School networks and content filters &
A managed school network may block the Squigglr website or the outside
services it calls (ASM-013). Lessons then fail to load in class, or saves
and refinement fail. &
The documentation for school administrators lists what must be allowed
(Table~\ref{tab:user-docs}); a failed refinement leaves the drawing
unchanged (G.4); unsaved attempts are kept and reported (SAF-RAY-003). \\
\addlinespace
New outside services &
Squigglr adds services that homes and schools did not depend on before: a
hosting service, an authentication service for adult accounts and,
possibly, an outside model for refinement (SRC-002). An outage, price
change or change of terms at any of them affects every user at once, and a
drawing sent to an outside model leaves the team's control. &
The interfaces are specified in Requirements for Interfacing with Adjacent
Systems and the data sent outside in Privacy Requirements; the refinement
provider can sit behind one interface (LC-004); costs are in
Section~\ref{sec:costs}. \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsection{Potential User Problems}
Squigglr has no existing users, so this subsection lists adverse reactions
its intended users may have, drawn from the personas (PER-1 to PER-3) and
the assumptions in Section~\ref{sec:assumptions}.
Table~\ref{tab:new-problems-users} gives each reaction and the precaution
taken.

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.39\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.35\textwidth}@{}}
\caption{New problems: potential user problems}
\label{tab:new-problems-users}\\
\toprule
\textbf{Problem (user)} & \textbf{Cause and effect} &
\textbf{Precaution and links} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Problem (user)} & \textbf{Cause and effect} &
\textbf{Precaution and links} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot
Frustration from repetition (child) &
Failing the same stage again and again, or restarting a lesson at once
after it ends, can agitate a child who dislikes repeated tasks (PER-1).
The child may become distressed or refuse the activity later. &
A lesson ends at \texttt{ATTEMPT\_LIMIT} (G.3, BR-002), and the adult
sets the limits for each child (BR-008). Hazards: H-RAY-004,
H-RAY-005. \\
\addlinespace
Reaction to sensory feedback (child) &
Sounds, colours or animation used as feedback may upset some children,
who react to sensory feedback in different ways (ASM-015). &
Audio can be turned off (SRC-005, \intref{P2}{37:39}); feedback is to be
designed with educators and occupational therapists (LC-005) and is
covered by the Usability and Humanity Requirements. \\
\addlinespace
Refinement that does not feel like the child's own (child) &
Refinement is automatic (BR-005), so the drawing changes without the child
asking. Whether children value this, and whether they still see the result
as theirs, is unverified (ASM-011, FACT-013). A child may be upset by the
change or not know how to get the original back. &
Refinement is minimal, within \texttt{REFINE\_TOLERANCE}, and the result
stays editable; the child, or an adult, can restore the original or turn
refinement off (BR-005, G.4, UC-007, UC-010). The scope of G.4 is OI-03. Hazards: H-MUH-001, H-MUH-002,
H-MUH-006. \\
\addlinespace
Summaries read as a diagnosis (adult) &
Adults reading records are not assumed to have training in visual-motor
development (CA-005), and fewer attempts may be read as better skill. An
adult may draw a wrong conclusion about the child and pass it on. &
No record or summary gives a diagnosis, judgement or comparison (BR-006,
SAF-RAY-004); the documentation explains how to read a summary
(Table~\ref{tab:user-docs}). Hazard: H-RAY-003. \\
\addlinespace
Over-trust in default values (adult) &
An adult may keep the default values, or pick values from the example
drawings, without knowing whether they suit the child (CA-006, ASM-016).
The child may be held at a stage too long or moved on too soon, and the
records may then mislead the adult. &
Example drawings show each end of the \texttt{STAGE\_PASS} range
(BR-008); the values in use are shown with the records (ASM-006); the
ranges are OI-05. Hazard: H-RAY-007. \\
\addlinespace
Worry about the child's data (adult) &
Parents want their child's data kept private (PER-2), and how records are
shared or deleted is not yet decided (OI-02, FACT-013). Adults may refuse
to use Squigglr, or share records more widely than they intend. &
A learner profile holds only a first name and a non-identifying username
(BR-001); privacy requirements follow from OI-02; a privacy notice is
part of the user documentation (Table~\ref{tab:user-docs}). \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsection{Limitations in the Anticipated Implementation Environment That May
Inhibit the New Product}
Table~\ref{tab:new-problems-limits} lists the limits of the environment
that Squigglr is built for and used in that may stop it from working as
specified. Several are the implementation risks the Development Plan's
Proof of Concept (POC) is designed to test (SRC-002).

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.39\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.35\textwidth}@{}}
\caption{New problems: limitations in the implementation environment}
\label{tab:new-problems-limits}\\
\toprule
\textbf{Limitation} & \textbf{Cause and effect} &
\textbf{Precaution and links} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Limitation} & \textbf{Cause and effect} &
\textbf{Precaution and links} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot
Stylus input in the browser &
Whether supported browsers report stylus strokes in enough detail is
untested (ASM-004). Stage checks and records could be wrong; if browser
input proves unreliable, an installed app may be needed, which would change
CON-001 (ULC-001). &
A POC experiment draws on a target tablet and on a second device and
browser combination and records any input failures (SRC-002); the
supported combinations are OI-09. Hazards: H-AHM-001, H-AHM-003; safety
requirement SAF-AHM-002. \\
\addlinespace
Devices without a stylus &
Not every home or school may have a touchscreen device with a stylus
(ASM-003). Children then draw with a finger, for which the stage checks
and ranges are not tuned. &
Finger input is a stretch goal (LC-006); SAF-RAY-002 tells the adult when
a device is not supported. \\
\addlinespace
Online use only &
Squigglr needs a network connection to load lessons and to save and view
records, and offline use is out of scope (ASM-005); a connection may be
lost at any time (CA-003). Attempts may be lost, and an adult may decide
from incomplete records. &
SAF-RAY-003 keeps unsaved attempts and reports them; network loss is
covered by the Robustness or Fault-Tolerance Requirements. Hazards:
H-RAY-002, H-MUH-005, H-MUH-008. \\
\addlinespace
Limits of free service plans &
The planned hosting service (Vercel) and database and authentication
service (Supabase) (SRC-002) have free plans with usage limits. The
hosting free plan is for personal, non-commercial use and has one
developer seat \citep{Vercel2026Pricing}. The database free plan includes
a 500\,MB database and pauses a project after one week of inactivity
\citep{Supabase2026Pricing}. A limit reached, or a project paused during a
break, makes Squigglr unavailable or stops saves. &
The Development Plan requires service limits and costs to be monitored
(SRC-002); the plan choice and its cost are in Section~\ref{sec:costs};
the budget is OI-07. \\
\addlinespace
Refinement model not yet chosen &
No model has been selected. A pretrained model may return invalid drawing
instructions, misread the drawing or change it too much, and its response
time and request cost are unknown (SRC-002). &
A POC experiment records valid and invalid outputs, response times and
estimated request costs, and checks that a rejected instruction leaves the
drawing unchanged; geometric refinement can be used while model work is
deferred (SRC-002). Links: LC-004, OI-03; hazard H-MUH-002. \\
\addlinespace
No testing with children &
No study with children may run (CON-006), so value ranges and
\texttt{REFINE\_TOLERANCE} are set from adult-made drawings (BR-009,
ASM-014). They may not match how children draw. &
The adult chooses each value for the child within a range (BR-008);
ASM-014 is marked relevant to safety and is an input to the Hazard
Analysis. \\
\addlinespace
Course schedule and effort &
The course fixes the deadlines (CON-004), and the team plans around about
ten hours per member per week (SRC-002). Not every planned feature may be
ready for each release. &
The POC plan allows narrowing the supported drawing set, simplifying the
guidance rules or using geometric refinement only (SRC-002); the phases
are set in Planning of the Development Phases. \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsection{Follow-Up Problems}
Follow-up problems are situations that Squigglr may create later and that
the team may not be able to handle. Table~\ref{tab:new-problems-follow-up}
lists them with the precaution or the open item that covers each.

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.39\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.35\textwidth}@{}}
\caption{New problems: follow-up problems}
\label{tab:new-problems-follow-up}\\
\toprule
\textbf{Problem} & \textbf{Cause and effect} &
\textbf{Precaution and links} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Problem} & \textbf{Cause and effect} &
\textbf{Precaution and links} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot
Demand for stretch features &
Adults or schools may ask for finger input, camera finger tracing or
letter activities, which are stretch goals (SRC-001; FACT-006; ASM-010).
The team may face pressure to add features the schedule cannot support,
or to add them without the checks the core features have. &
Deferred features are kept in the Waiting Room section; new input types
can be added through one input module (LC-003, LC-006). \\
\addlinespace
A growing lesson catalogue &
Lessons may be added or reordered, for example to add letters after shapes
(LC-001, SRC-009.04). Each new lesson needs its stages, predefined difficulties
and adult-made example drawings for \texttt{STAGE\_PASS} (BR-008,
BR-009), so content work grows with every lesson. &
Lessons and their stages can be stored as data, so adding or reordering a
lesson changes no code (LC-001). \\
\addlinespace
Growing stored data &
Every attempt keeps the child's actual strokes (BR-004) and every recorded
value (LC-007), and each creative drawing keeps its original strokes
(BR-005). Storage grows with use and could reach the database free plan's
500\,MB limit \citep{Supabase2026Pricing}, after which saves could
fail. &
Capacity is covered by the Capacity Requirements; storage use is monitored
(SRC-002); failed saves are reported (SAF-RAY-003); how long records are
kept follows from OI-02. \\
\addlinespace
Use by institutions &
Schools or centres may adopt Squigglr; a peer reviewer asked how their
records would be kept apart
(\href{https://github.com/AHMEDELZARIA/squigglr/issues/75}{\#75}), and an
educator may manage many learners (ASM-012). One shared store of records
may not meet an institution's expectations. &
OI-06 decides whether separate institutions are supported in this project
and, if so, the access and privacy requirements that keep their records
apart. \\
\addlinespace
Maintenance after the course &
Who maintains Squigglr after the project ends is not decided (Maintenance
Users and Service Technicians). Hosting accounts, provider access and
lesson content may lapse while adults' accounts and children's practice
records stay stored with no one responsible for them. &
Who maintains Squigglr after the project ends is an open item (Maintenance
Users and Service Technicians), and how stored records are deleted is part
of OI-02. \\
\addlinespace
Changes by outside providers &
Providers may change prices, limits or terms; one vendor states that its
pricing may change \citep{Supabase2026Pricing}. A refinement provider may
change or withdraw a model (LC-004). Costs may then rise above the course
limit, or refinement may stop working. &
The refinement provider can sit behind one interface (LC-004), with
geometric refinement as a fallback (SRC-002); the budget is OI-07. \\
\addlinespace
Pressure to evaluate children &
If Squigglr is presented as an educational tool rather than as practice,
adults or schools may expect it to evaluate children. The supervisor
advised that any evaluation would need strong justification, because the
team cannot judge a child's abilities (SRC-008). &
Squigglr gives no diagnosis, judgement or comparison (BR-006, ULC-004). \\
\addlinespace
Laws that may apply after wider use &
Wider use, for example by schools or outside the course, may bring legal
obligations about records of children that the project has not had to
meet. &
Which laws and standards apply is decided in the Legal Requirements
section; this section assumes none. \\
\end{longtable}
}

\section{Tasks}
\label{sec:tasks}
\subsection{Project Planning}

The Development Plan (\srcref{002}) defines the project's schedule, team
responsibilities, development process, communication practices and deliverable
management. Those arrangements are maintained in the Development Plan rather
than duplicated in the SRS. This section assigns the SRS requirements to
delivery phases; the course deadlines also appear under CON-004
(Table~\ref{tab:con-schedule}).

\subsection{Planning of the Development Phases}
\label{sec:development-phases}

The delivery plan distinguishes the following requirement targets:
\begin{description}[style=nextline]
  \item[PH-1 --- Proof of Concept (PoC), November 23, 2026.]
  A demonstration of the core guided lesson flow, input capture, stage
  classification and pass-based progression using prepared examples.
  \item[PH-2 --- Revision 0, February 1, 2027.]
  The learner-specific configuration, feedback, retry and stopping rules,
  and drawing-input and session safeguards required for a usable revision.
  \item[PH-3 --- Final demonstration, March 15, 2027.]
  The remaining record-history and progress-presentation safeguards and
  final integrated functionality.
  \item[Future.]
  Enhancements outside the committed capstone delivery phases, recorded
  in Section~\ref{sec:waiting-room}.
\end{description}

\emph{Priority} describes the requirement's delivery importance
(\emph{Must}, \emph{Should} or \emph{Could}); \emph{phase} states the
first milestone at which that behaviour is targeted. A PH-1 capability
remains part of PH-2 and PH-3. The phase classification does not relax any
safety requirement when its associated feature is in use: a phase
demonstration shall not rely on a safeguard that has not been implemented
for real learner use.

Table~\ref{tab:requirement-phases} lists every currently specified SRS
requirement, including the safety requirements derived from the Hazard
Analysis. The functional-requirement priorities and phases agree with
Section~\ref{sec:fr-learning}; the Hazard Analysis-derived requirements
are classified here as \emph{Must} and assigned to the phase containing
the behaviour they protect. The 33 identified requirements in this SRS are scheduled in PH-1
through PH-3. Candidate enhancements are assigned to Future in
Section~\ref{sec:waiting-room}.

\texttt{SAF-RAY-007} & Active-learner identification and isolation & Must & PH-2 \\
\addlinespace
\texttt{SAF-RAY-008} & Adult-setting bounds and authorization & Must & PH-2 \\
\addlinespace
\texttt{SAF-AHM-001} & Duplicate input-event integrity & Must & PH-2 \\
\addlinespace
\texttt{SAF-AHM-002} & Drawing-coordinate integrity & Must & PH-2 \\
\end{longtable}
}

The phase allocation is a planning baseline rather than an alternative
source of requirement definitions. If a requirement's scope or phase
changes, its entry in this table and, for requirements with a
\texttt{Phase} field, its individual specification are updated together.

\section{Migration to the New Product}
\label{sec:migration}
This section asks what must be converted or carried over before Squigglr
can be used. Squigglr is a new product (Implementation Environment of the
Current System), so both subsections find nothing to migrate. Each gives
its reason and the condition that would change the answer.

\subsection{Requirements for Migration to the New Product}
Not applicable. There is no existing product to convert, decommission or
run in parallel, because Squigglr is new and replaces no system. Getting
started is part of the product, not a migration task: an adult creates a
learner profile and chooses the adult-set values (UC-001, BR-001,
BR-008), and these steps are covered by the use cases and by the
user documentation in Section~\ref{sec:user-docs}. Between the releases
set in Planning of the Development Phases, Squigglr holds only prepared or
adult-made data (CON-005, BR-009, ASM-014). Because every release is
tested again in full (SRC-008), that data can be created again for each
release instead of being converted. If adults keep real records in one
release and a later release changes how records are stored, for example to
show new values (LC-007) or to measure improvement differently (LC-008),
migrating those records would become a requirement here.

\subsection{Data That Has to be Modified or Translated for the New System}
Not applicable. No data held elsewhere is imported into Squigglr: no use
case from UC-001 to UC-010 enters children's earlier drawings or adults'
own notes. Every attempt record, lesson record and creative drawing is
created inside Squigglr as the child practises (G.4, G.5), so nothing has
to be translated into a new format.

limit, of which at most \$150 can be reimbursed. Whether any budget is

\section{User Documentation and Training}
\label{sec:user-docs}
This section describes the user documentation and training Squigglr needs,
not the documents themselves. Following the Volere template
\citep{robertson2012volere}, each item is derived from the use cases
(UC-001 to UC-010) and the business rules and assumptions the adult must
understand to carry them out.

\subsection{User Documentation Requirements}
Table~\ref{tab:user-docs} lists the documentation supporting adults need.
Every use case from UC-001 to UC-010 is covered by at least one item.

Getting started &
Create a learner profile that holds only a first name and
a non-identifying username. Choose each adult-set value: what it means,
its range, the example drawings for \texttt{STAGE\_PASS}, and that a value
not chosen is set to the middle of its range, which may not suit the
child. Change the values later. &

Lessons and stages &
The lesson catalogue; the stages from demonstration to drawing with no
guide; choosing a lesson and a starting stage; what happens when a lesson
ends at \texttt{ATTEMPT\_LIMIT} or is extended while the child

That refinement happens automatically and changes the drawing only a

}

Each item appears as help inside the adult views, where the task is done,
and is collected in a written User Guide, which the course template
provides as a separate document. As the Volere template advises, the
guide uses the terms defined in this specification and turns the use case
scenarios into its step-by-step instructions. The guide is written in the
interface language (ASM-007). Developer documents, such as the design
documents, are not user documentation and are not listed.

Children receive no written documentation, because many cannot read
(ASM-007). Instead, each lesson starts with a demonstration (G.1), and
children move through Squigglr using images and icons rather than text
(FACT-007; SRC-005, \intref{P1}{34:13}). How quickly children and adults
must be able to learn Squigglr is specified in Learning Requirements and
is not repeated here.

The user interface and user experience (UI/UX) Lead coordinates the
documentation, as the lead for user flows
and usability (SRC-002). Each item is first written for the release that
delivers the use cases it covers, and is updated whenever those use cases,
the lesson catalogue (LC-001) or the adult-set values (LC-002) change.
Who keeps it current after the project ends follows the maintenance
decision in Table~\ref{tab:new-problems-follow-up}. No documentation
requirement is stated here: the behaviour the documentation explains is
already specified by the use cases and business rules, the practice-only
statement is SAF-RAY-004, and learnability is in Learning Requirements, so
a separate requirement would repeat them.

\subsection{Training Requirements}
No training sessions are planned for any user. Supporting adults are
assumed to be everyday web users (Hands-On Users of the Project), and the
documentation in Table~\ref{tab:user-docs}, shown where each task is done,
is meant to let them set up and use Squigglr without help. Whether it is
enough is checked, not assumed: the adult-reviewer tasks in the G.6
measurement and the planned usability sessions with adult reviewers
(SRC-001) test whether adults can complete their tasks with only this
help. Children receive no training beyond the demonstration that starts
each lesson (G.1). The adult introduces Squigglr to the child, and the
getting-started item suggests how to begin a first session, for example by
choosing an early stage and checking the audio setting. If schools adopt
Squigglr and an educator manages many learners (ASM-012), a short
walkthrough for educators may be needed; it is not planned in this
project.

Their classification as \emph{Future} is not a requirement to implement

Creative space refinement remains within goal G.4 unless the team
formally changes that goal (OI-03); it is not moved into the waiting

promised as future features.

\section{Ideas for Solution}
\label{sec:solution-ideas}
This section is non-normative: it records ideas for how the team might
build Squigglr, so that they are not lost and are not mistaken for
requirements; none of them binds the design. Ready-made products and
components that could be reused are in the Off-the-Shelf Solutions
section; this section is about how the team might put the product
together. Table~\ref{tab:solution-ideas} gives each idea with its
trade-off and source.

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.26\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.44\textwidth}%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.22\textwidth}@{}}
\caption{Ideas for solution (non-normative)}\label{tab:solution-ideas}\\
\toprule
\textbf{Idea} & \textbf{Trade-off} & \textbf{Source} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Idea} & \textbf{Trade-off} & \textbf{Source} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot
\emph{Geometric refinement first.} Smooth and simplify the child's own
paths, and try a pretrained model only for changes that geometry cannot
make. &
No outside service, request cost or data leaving Squigglr, and results are
predictable. Geometry can only tidy strokes, not interpret a drawing, and
the supervisor's question of whether AI is needed (SRC-004) stays open
until POC evidence exists. &
SRC-002 (Drawing Assessment and Enhancement; POC experiments); SRC-004 \\
\addlinespace
\emph{Check model output before applying it.} Treat each model response
as untrusted: apply it only if every operation is from a known set, refers
to the current drawing and keeps every point within
\texttt{REFINE\_TOLERANCE}; otherwise leave the drawing unchanged. &
Adds checking logic and rejects some useful responses, but makes the G.4
check that a failed refinement leaves the drawing unchanged simple to
test. &
SRC-002; G.4; H-MUH-002 \\
\addlinespace
\emph{Keep the original strokes apart.} Store the child's original
strokes separately from the refined drawing, so that restoring switches
back to them rather than undoing each change. &
More stored data per creative drawing (Growing stored data,
Table~\ref{tab:new-problems-follow-up}); restoring is exact however many
edits followed. &
SRC-002; BR-005; H-MUH-001, H-MUH-003 \\
\addlinespace
\emph{One refinement interface.} Put every refinement method behind one
interface that takes stroke coordinates and returns adjusted coordinates
at the same scale. &
A provider or a geometric method can be swapped in one module. An
interface that returns only adjusted coordinates cannot express adding a
shape, which the Development Plan lists as a candidate model instruction;
adding content would also go beyond minimal refinement (BR-005). &
LC-004; SRC-002 \\
\addlinespace
\emph{Lessons and feedback as content.} Store lessons, stages, predefined
difficulties, example drawings and feedback wording as data that the
lesson logic reads. &
Adding a lesson or rewording feedback changes no code, but the data needs
its own checks so that no lesson is released without its difficulties or
example drawings. &
LC-001, LC-005; BR-008, BR-009 \\
\addlinespace
\emph{One input module.} Turn browser pointer events into strokes in one
place, so that the rest of Squigglr sees only strokes. &
A new device or finger input affects one module, but that module must be
tested on each supported device, because emulation cannot replace stylus
testing (SRC-002). &
LC-003, LC-006; SRC-002 \\
\addlinespace
\emph{Local queue for unsaved attempts.} Hold each completed attempt in
the browser until the server confirms the save, then delete the local
copy. &
Keeps attempts through a lost connection (CA-003), but copies left on a
shared device can be seen by the next user (H-RAY-006), so when they are
cleared must be settled with OI-01. &
SAF-RAY-003; CA-003; OI-01 \\
\addlinespace
\emph{Spending controls.} Use the vendors' own controls: the database
service turns a spend cap on by default on its paid plan
\citep{Supabase2026Pricing}, and the hosting service gives each new team a
usage budget with notifications and can pause all projects when it is
reached \citep{Vercel2026Pricing}. &
Keeps spending within the course limit (Section~\ref{sec:costs}), but a
reached cap can pause the service, which is the availability problem in
Table~\ref{tab:new-problems-limits}. &
Vendor pages; OI-07 \\
\addlinespace
\emph{Model on university computing.} Run a pretrained model on the
department's graphics processing unit (GPU) cluster, which the course
outline says is available for large language model computing, instead of
a paid outside service. &
No per-request fee, and drawings stay with the university. The team would
run the model itself, and whether a public website may call the cluster,
and for how long after the course, is unknown. &
\citep{Smith2026Outline}; SRC-002 (no custom model training planned) \\
\end{longtable}
}

\section{Traceability}
\label{sec:traceability}

This section provides three traceability views of the requirements links:
sources through product use cases to requirements
(Section~\ref{sec:goal-uc-req}), functional-to-nonfunctional
links (Section~\ref{sec:fr-nfr}), and hazards to their mitigating
requirements (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-req}). Entries are based on the
requirements' own \emph{Sources}, \emph{Traceability} and \emph{Hazards}
fields and on the corresponding Product Use Case and FMEA tables. Discussion 
of similar behaviour does not imply a requirements link.

\subsection{Goal/source to use case to requirement}
\label{sec:goal-uc-req}

\section{Appendix --- Requirement Sources}
\label{app:sources}

This appendix is the requirement sources register. Each source has a fixed
identifier (SRC-001 to SRC-009) that is never renumbered. A requirement names
the sources it comes from in its rationale or source field. A statement from
the specialist interview is cited by its source identifier, participant code
and transcript timestamp, for example ``(SRC-005, \intref{P1}{28:37})''; the statement
identifiers in Section~\ref{sec:src-interview} (for example SRC-005.01) can
be cited instead. The interview is expert-reported evidence only. The three
participants are de-identified domain specialists (P1, P2 and P3). No child
took part in the interview or in any other source listed here.

Table~\ref{tab:src-docs} lists the project documents and meeting sources, and
Table~\ref{tab:src-other} lists the interview, questionnaire and peer-review sources. Requirements identify their supporting sources through stable source
identifiers. Each source and statement has a \LaTeX{} label
(for example \texttt{src:005.01}), so a requirement can link to it and the
link works in the PDF.

The sources represent users in different ways. Children are the main
beneficiaries, but by the supervisor's direction (SRC-004) no requirement is
gathered from children and no information about them is collected. Their
needs enter the requirements only through adults who know them. The domain
specialists' interview and questionnaire (SRC-005, SRC-009) are the
outside evidence for these requirements. Their advice established adult control of lesson selection and optional
stage changes, stylus as the main input, finger input as a stretch goal,
and camera tracing as a stretch goal. Parents, educators and occupational therapists have not been
consulted yet. The specialists advised doing so (SRC-005.03, SRC-005.06), and
until then the requirements that depend on their views rest on the expert
statements below and are open to change.

\subsection{Key Interview Statements}
\label{sec:src-interview}

This subsection lists the interview statements most relevant to the
requirements. Each is a short paraphrase, not a quote; the exact words are in
Appendix~\ref{app:interview} at the given timestamp.

\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em, style=nextline]
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.01}SRC-005.01 (\intref{P1}{28:37}; \intref{P2}{29:18}; \intlink{Rayan}{30:20}{30:20--31:02})}]
Deciding automatically when a child is ready for the next level would need
assessment and is too complex for the time frame. An adult who knows the
child (a parent or educator) should choose the level. The team agreed to
leave progression to the supervising adult.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.02}SRC-005.02 (\intref{P2}{29:18})}]
In a past project, a teacher personalized each child's activities through a
separate adult interface that also showed progress.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.03}SRC-005.03 (\intref{P2}{31:42})}]
Which progress measures help an adult should be decided with occupational
therapists or educational specialists, using milestones from the literature
if they exist.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.04}SRC-005.04 (\intref{P3}{24:34}; \intref{P3}{25:36}; \intref{P1}{26:46})}]
The specialists disagreed on input. P3 said that how a child holds a pen
affects how they draw and the usability of the app, and suggested finger
input unless fine motor practice is the goal; R2 later agreed that finger
drawing may be simpler (\srcref{009.03}). P1 agreed that a finger is the
easiest start, but said fine motor practice needs a held tool, moving from
a larger stylus to a smaller one as later difficulty levels.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.05}SRC-005.05 (\intref{P1}{34:13})}]
The app should be child-friendly: a child who cannot read yet should be able
to enter a lesson, practise, restart and move back and forth using images
and icons rather than text, without constant adult help. Adults handle setup.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.06}SRC-005.06 (\intref{P1}{36:26})}]
Positive feedback wording and colours should be designed with feedback from
educators and occupational therapists, built up bit by bit.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.07}SRC-005.07 (\intref{P2}{37:39})}]
Audio feedback is a good idea; offering more than one modality is
encouraged in inclusive design, with the user able to turn the audio off.
Design it carefully with therapists' feedback, and consider adults with ADHD
or autism as reviewers when children cannot take part.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.08}SRC-005.08 (\intref{P2}{39:26})}]
Start with the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG), then apply more
specialized design heuristics for autism from the literature.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.09}SRC-005.09 (\intref{P2}{19:05}; \intref{P1}{20:27}; \intref{P2}{20:47})}]
From a universal design view, the skills apply to all children, including
those with motor or sight challenges; designing for disability serves many
groups.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.10}SRC-005.10 (\intref{P1}{13:42}; \intref{P1}{15:12}; \intref{P1}{16:13}; \intref{P2}{16:45})}]
Camera finger tracing in the air practises gross rather than fine motor
skills. It is useful but should come after the core touchscreen tracing
when time is limited.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.11}SRC-005.11 (\intref{P2}{22:56}; \intref{P1}{24:03}; \intref{P1}{33:00})}]
Testing with children needs ethics approval (about three to four months
where P2 works). Meeting experts for feedback without involving children
does not.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.12}SRC-005.12 (\intlink{Rayan}{41:51}{41:51--42:48})}]
All three participants agreed to the use of the transcript for eliciting
requirements.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:005.13}SRC-005.13 (\intref{P2}{35:36})}]
How the child and the caregiver share the app, for example with the child
as the user and the caregiver giving access, is the team's design decision;
there is no single right workflow. Many drawing apps for children already
exist.
\end{description}

\subsection{Key Questionnaire Statements}
\label{sec:src-questionnaire}

This subsection lists the questionnaire statements most relevant to the
requirements. Each is a short paraphrase; the full answers are in
Appendix~\ref{app:questionnaire}, labelled by respondent (R1, R2) and
question (Q1--Q4).

\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em, style=nextline]
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:009.01}SRC-009.01 (\hyperref[q:R1-Q1]{R1, Q1}; \hyperref[q:R2-Q1]{R2, Q1})}]
The free drawing space is valuable. Refinement should stay subtle and
support the child's own drawing rather than replace or correct it, so the
child keeps ownership. Both respondents suggested that refinement be
optional or adjustable.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:009.02}SRC-009.02 (\hyperref[q:R1-Q2]{R1, Q2})}]
Activities motivate when they are achievable, predictable and visually
engaging, with immediate positive feedback and some choice. They frustrate
when they are too demanding, repeatedly highlight mistakes, need very
precise movements or change the child's work unexpectedly. Squigglr should
recognize effort and progress, not only accuracy.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:009.03}SRC-009.03 (\hyperref[q:R2-Q2]{R2, Q2})}]
Requiring a pen or stylus rather than a finger may make drawing harder;
finger drawing may be simpler.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:009.04}SRC-009.04 (\hyperref[q:R1-Q3]{R1, Q3}; \hyperref[q:R2-Q3]{R2, Q3})}]
Letters are a useful later stage, introduced gradually after shapes, with
some flexibility in how precisely a letter must be traced. R1 suggested
Arabic as well as English letters; R2 suggested letters and numbers, then
tracing letters to form a word.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:009.05}SRC-009.05 (\hyperref[q:R2-Q4]{R2, Q4})}]
The interface should be appealing and child-friendly while avoiding
excessive visual or auditory stimuli.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:009.06}SRC-009.06 (\hyperref[q:R1-Q4]{R1, Q4})}]
Involve children, parents, educators and occupational therapists in design
and evaluation, and look beyond accuracy at engagement, frustration,
independence, confidence and transfer to drawing without the app.
\item[{\phantomsection%
  \label{src:009.07}SRC-009.07 (\hyperref[q:R1-Q4]{R1, Q4})}]
Squigglr could serve a broader population, such as other
neurodevelopmental conditions and rehabilitation; avoid restricting it to
one condition too early.
\end{description}

\subsection*{Individual Disclosures}

\textbf{Rayan:} I used Anthropic's Claude Opus 5.5 at medium effort,
accessed through Claude Code, while preparing my sections of this document.
I developed the ideas and made the project decisions in my sections myself,
and Moustafa and I conducted the specialist interview and follow-up
questionnaire. A major use of AI was organizing our sources: I used the
model to sort through the interview transcript, questionnaire answers, and
meeting notes, and to extract the specific statements, with their
timestamps, that supported the ideas I wanted to include. This would have
been very difficult to do by hand given the length of the material. I also
used it to turn my notes and decisions into draft wording, to improve
sentence structure, clarity, and flow, and to help with \LaTeX{}
formatting. Through Claude Code, it also helped manage the repository
(branches, commits, and merges) and helped me review my teammates' pull
requests by checking their changes against our decisions and the rest of
the document; I decided which review comments to post.

All ideas and decisions in my sections are my own. I reviewed every
AI-assisted passage against my intended meaning and checked each cited
statement against the original transcript, questionnaire, or meeting notes
to confirm that it says what we claim it says.

\textbf{Ahmed:} I used OpenAI's ChatGPT (GPT-6) while preparing my
sections of the SRS, particularly the document structure, learning
activity requirements, formal lesson specification and development-phase
planning. All the ideas behind my contributions are my own, and project
decisions were made through discussion with my teammates. I used AI to
help express those ideas in a clear and grammatically correct way,
including turning my notes into requirement statements, improving
sentence structure and making technical explanations easier to follow.
It also helped with \LaTeX{} formatting and with checking the clarity
of section references and wording across related requirements. Our team 
also used AI to help divide the workload fairly and evenly among members, 
taking into account the scope and complexity of each task. I personally used it 
to help write clear and descriptive pull request descriptions that accurately 
summarized the changes I made, making them easier for my teammates to review.

I reviewed the AI-assisted text to make sure it expressed the decisions
we had made and stayed consistent with the SRS, our meeting discussions
and the parts of the document written by other team members. AI was a
writing and formatting aid; the final content and decisions remained
my responsibility and the team's.

\textbf{Shamil:} For the SRS I used the Kiro IDE which uses automatically 
selects an optimal LLM for consistency among multiple versions of Claude,
along with VS Code Copilot which uses ChatGPT 6 Luna. I used the chatbot 
to generate a skeletons of my sections with starting text based on analysis 
of the document. I used this starting text for idea generation and brainstorming, 
using my own words for the written content, but sometimes repurposing generated 
text with rewording, paraphrasing, or by completely rewriting. For short standalone 
sentences I may have kept them as they are. When rewriting, I removed sections based 
on false assumptions and made sure it was consistent with the rest of the 
document. None of the text for the reflection questions is AI-generated. 
I also used AI to make citations for compliance documents in References.bib
and generate a starting table for the traceability section.
All decisions in my sections are my own and I reviewed all AI-assisted 
passages.

\textbf{Moustafa:} I used Anthropic's Claude Opus 5.5, accessed through
Claude Code, alternating between medium and high effort depending on the
task. Because the document was so large, I relied on it heavily to save
time on project management. Connected to the GitHub CLI, it split my
assignment into ten sub-issues so I could track my work in smaller pieces,
checked which tasks I still had left, and made my commits and pull
requests. For writing, I decided what each part should say and gave it my
ideas, sometimes after brainstorming with it. It then wrote a first draft
and the \LaTeX{} for it, which I refined until it said exactly what I
intended. In this document, that covered Sections 6, 8, 10 and 11, the
SAF-MOU safety requirements in Section 12.2, and my changes to Appendix A.
I also used it for review: as teammates merged their sections, I had it
read the document again to check that my additions stayed consistent with
the rest and contradicted nothing, and it helped me review my teammates'
pull requests. GitHub Copilot also gave a light automated review on a few
of my pull requests.

All ideas in my sections are my own. I reviewed every AI-drafted passage
against my intended meaning before including it, and I decided which
flagged inconsistencies and review comments were valid and how to resolve
them.

\newpage{}

\section{Appendix --- Reflection}
\label{app:reflection}

\input{../Reflection.text}

\begin{enumerate}

\item What went well while writing this deliverable?

\textbf{Rayan:} What went well was that most of my sections could be built on
what our stakeholders had actually told us, rather than on our own guesses.
Moustafa and I interviewed three domain specialists on October 1, 2026, and
the de-identified transcript is now an appendix to the SRS. Because of this,
Sections 1, 2 and 5 and the requirement sources in Appendix A cite the
specialists (P1 to P3) by timestamp, so a reader can check exactly where each
statement came from. Our meetings with our supervisor, Dr.~Anand, on September
23 and October 14, 2026, and with our TA on September 23 and October 7, 2026,
gave us the same kind of traceable source for the constraints and business
rules. It also helped that I split my part of the SRS into one issue and one
pull request per section, which kept each review small and let each section be
checked and merged on its own. The main thing I took from this is that
recording sources as we go makes the requirements much easier to justify and
to change later.

\textbf{Ahmed:} What went well for me was being able to build the
learning activity requirements around a flow we had already discussed
as a team. We knew that an adult would choose the lesson and starting
stage, while the child would move through progressively less guided
drawing stages based on their attempts. Having those decisions in place
made it easier to write requirements that describe exactly what the
application should do instead of leaving the behaviour open to
interpretation.

I also think splitting my work into smaller, focused contributions
helped a lot. I worked on the SRS structure first, then the learning
requirements and formal specification, and later the development
phases. That gave the team something specific to review each time. The
formal specification was especially useful because it forced me to
think through cases like failed attempts, improvement extensions and
adult-approved changes. I could then compare those rules with the
written requirements and the hazards in the other document. It was
satisfying to see the different sections start fitting together rather
than feeling like separate pieces of writing.

\textbf{Moustafa:} What went well was that we did most of the thinking
before we started writing. Rayan and I interviewed three domain specialists
on October 1, and they later filled out a follow-up questionnaire for us.
Along with our meetings with our supervisor, Dr.~Anand, and our TA, that
shaped a lot of the app before we assumed anything ourselves. For example,
the specialists are why the adult decides when a child moves on, and
Dr.~Anand is why adults set the thresholds within a range we define. So
when it came time to write my sections, it felt more like organizing than
coming up with things from scratch. For most requirements I could just ask
myself what the specialists, Dr.~Anand or the TA had said, and put it
there with the source.

\item What pain points did you experience during this deliverable, and how did
you resolve them?

\textbf{Rayan:} The main pain point was that decisions kept changing as we
learned more, and each change affected several sections at once. In our
interview on October 1, 2026, the specialists said that the supporting adult,
not the app, should decide when a child moves to an easier or harder stage, so
we changed our progression goal from automatic to adult-controlled. In our
meeting on October 14, 2026, Dr.~Anand advised that thresholds such as the
pass criteria and the number of failed attempts should be set by the adult for
each child within a range the team defines, which changed several constraints,
business rules and goals. The answers to the follow-up questionnaire that
Moustafa and I sent the specialists, returned between October 2 and 4, 2026,
then confirmed that letters belong in a later stage after shapes, so we kept
them as a stretch goal and recorded the order of stages as a likely change. I resolved
this by updating every affected section in the same pull request as each
change, and by recording anything still unsettled in Open Issues (Section 18),
so that nothing was left half-changed. A smaller problem was that several of
my branches were open at once and each added a row to the revision history, so
later pull requests conflicted on that table; I merged main into each branch
and kept every row. I also found that some early drafts included details
nobody had actually discussed, so I went back through each section and removed
any claim I could not trace to a source or a team decision. Looking back, I
learned that requirements are never really finished early in a project, and
that keeping each change in one place and tracing it to its source is what
makes them manageable.

\textbf{Ahmed:} My biggest difficulty was keeping everything
consistent as the requirements changed. A rule that sounded simple in
a meeting often had several consequences once I tried to specify it.
For example, we had to distinguish between automatically moving to
the next stage after a passing drawing and changing lessons or moving
backward, which should require an adult's approval. We also had to
make sure that interrupted attempts did not count toward the attempt
limit and that a child could not keep receiving extensions indefinitely.
I ended up revisiting the same requirements and state transitions
multiple times to make those distinctions clear.

The \LaTeX{} and Git workflow was another challenge. Some section
references initially appeared as question marks until I checked the
labels and rebuilt the document, and integrating changes from multiple
branches meant I had to be careful not to replace newer work from my
teammates. One review caught an issue where a previous version would
have removed safety requirements that were already on main. I
corrected that and became much more deliberate about checking diffs
before submitting a pull request. It was a good reminder that writing
a correct section is only part of the job; it also has to integrate
safely into the team's document.

\textbf{Moustafa:} The biggest pain point was how long and repetitive the
document was. With five of us writing different sections, there was a good
chance two people were writing about the same thing in different places,
and even a slightly different idea of how something should work meant we
would contradict each other. As Ahmed and Rayan merged their sections while
I wrote mine, I kept noticing things that did not line up. For example, my
use cases had the child opening a lesson, while Rayan's assumptions said
only the adult starts one. Each time, one of us had to change our part, so
we would message or call to decide which version made more sense. Checking
a document this size by hand was really tedious, so we used AI to review
it and flag inconsistencies, and then we decided together which version was
right.

\item How many of your requirements were inspired by speaking to your
client(s) or their proxies (e.g. your peers, stakeholders, potential users)?

\textbf{Rayan:} In my sections, I wrote 24 requirements: 7 mandated
constraints, 9 business rules and 8 safety requirements. Of these, 18 cite at
least one conversation with our stakeholders or their proxies. Ten come from
our interview with the specialists on October 1, 2026, including that adults
choose the lesson and stage, that guided lesson records keep the child's
actual strokes, and that Squigglr gives no diagnosis. Eleven come from our
meetings with Dr.~Anand on September 23 and October 14, 2026, including that
Squigglr is a website, that no information is gathered from or about children,
and that the adult sets each child's thresholds within a team-set range. Some
cite both. The other six come from our own project documents and the course
requirements, such as the deliverable deadlines. Since none of these
stakeholders are the children themselves, nearly every requirement that
affects how a child uses Squigglr rests on what adults and specialists told
us, which is why we plan to check them with the domain specialists again later.

\textbf{Ahmed:} I contributed 25 individually identified
requirements: 23 learning activity requirements (FR-LRN-001 through
FR-LRN-023) and two safety-critical requirements (SAF-AHM-001 and
SAF-AHM-002). At least 13 of the learning requirements explicitly
cite the domain-specialist interview, follow-up questionnaire, or
meetings with our supervisor and TA as sources. For example, the
specialists' advice shaped the distinction between adult-controlled
lesson decisions and routine progression within a lesson. Our
supervisor's feedback about letting adults choose thresholds within
team-defined ranges also affected the pass, settings and
improvement-related requirements.

The remaining requirements mainly came from working through what was
needed to make those agreed behaviours reliable and testable. When we
specified stylus capture, for example, I had to account for multiple
strokes, interrupted input and incorrect classifications even though
nobody had handed us a detailed rule for each case. The two additional
safety requirements came from my failure-mode analysis of duplicate
stylus events and coordinate-mapping errors. I learned that talking
to stakeholders establishes the direction, but turning their needs
into a complete specification also requires careful engineering
judgement about failure cases and edge conditions.

\textbf{Moustafa:} I wrote 27 requirements: 24 look-and-feel and usability
requirements (NFR-UX-001 to NFR-UX-024) and three safety requirements
(SAF-MOU-001 to SAF-MOU-003). Of these, 25 cite something our stakeholders
or their proxies told us. Most come from the specialists. The interview
gave us things like icon-based navigation for kids who can't read yet,
positive feedback wording, and audio that can be turned off. The
questionnaire added that the app should stay calm and not overwhelming,
that refinement should be optional, and that feedback should recognize
effort. Our supervisor told us to borrow specific WCAG ideas, like large
icons, instead of treating WCAG as a constraint, which shaped the whole
accessibility section, and our TA's comment about defining jargon became
the plain-wording requirement for adult views. The two that don't cite
anyone, SAF-MOU-001 and SAF-MOU-003, came out of our hazard analysis.

\item Which of the courses you have taken, or are currently taking, will help
your team to be successful with your capstone project.

\textbf{Rayan:} Two courses help directly. The first is SFWRENG 4HC3,
Human-Computer Interaction, which I am taking now and where our group's term
project is Squigglr itself. The user research we do there, such as planning
interviews with caregivers and educators and building personas, feeds straight
into the stakeholders and requirements in this document. The second is
SFWRENG 3RA3, Software Requirements, which I took in my third year. The
requirements document we wrote for that course followed Meyer's template
rather than Volere, but the concepts carry over directly. In 3RA3 we learned
to separate hard goals, which need a clear, measurable and verifiable
satisfaction criterion, from soft goals, which have to be broken down until
each part can be measured. That is the same idea as the fit criterion Volere
attaches to each requirement. We also learned to check every requirement
against three qualities, that it is necessary, verifiable and attainable,
and to write requirements with the EARS sentence patterns, such as ``When an
event occurs, the system shall\ldots'', so that the wording stays precise.
Being able to transfer those concepts helped me a lot with this deliverable in
particular, because I already knew how to write goals and requirements that
are precise and checkable and only had to adapt them to a new template.

\textbf{Ahmed:} SFWRENG 4HC3, Human-Computer Interaction, has been
very relevant because it keeps bringing the discussion back to what
users actually need and how they will experience the product. The
work on personas, scenarios and requirements elicitation helped me
think about the difference between a child drawing in a lesson and an
adult setting it up or reviewing the results. That distinction became
important when writing our lesson-control requirements.

Our Machine Learning course has also been useful, even though
Squigglr is not meant to diagnose or assess children. Learning how
a model or classifier produces an output made me think more carefully
about what it means for a drawing to pass a fixed criterion and what
can go wrong if the classification is inaccurate. The formal models
and state-based reasoning we encounter in software engineering and
real-time systems are relevant too. I used that way of thinking when
writing the guided lesson transitions, such as exactly when the
system should advance, retry, suggest an earlier stage or end the
lesson. These courses helped me move from describing a feature to
specifying behaviour that somebody can actually verify.

\textbf{Moustafa:} Two courses help me the most. The first is SFWRENG 4HC3,
Human-Computer Interaction. A lot of what it teaches feels obvious at first,
but once you actually go through the concepts you realize how much thought
a good interface needs, and I had never thought about UI that deeply
before. That matters a lot for Squigglr, because it is built for young kids
and kids with disabilities, so being able to navigate the app easily is one
of the most important parts of the whole project. Most of my accessibility
and usability sections came from that way of thinking. The second is
SFWRENG 3A04, Software Design III: Large System Design. In that course we
worked with business use cases and wrote use cases with a main flow and
alternative flows, which is exactly what I did for the business use cases
and product use cases here. We did the same thing in 3RA3. 3A04 will also
help when we plan the architecture for the design document.

\item What knowledge and skills will the team collectively need to acquire to
successfully complete this capstone project? Examples of possible knowledge
to acquire include domain specific knowledge from the domain of your
application, or software engineering knowledge, mechatronics knowledge or
computer science knowledge. Skills may be related to technology, or writing,
or presentation, or team management, etc. You should look to identify at
least one item for each team member.

\textbf{Rayan:} The most important knowledge the team needs is domain
knowledge about the children Squigglr is for: how autistic children and
children with ADHD develop visual-motor skills, how they use apps, and what
helps or frustrates them. None of us has a background in this area, and it is
not something we could safely guess, because a wrong assumption could make
the app harder or more upsetting for the children it is meant to help. That
is why it was necessary to get input from people who have designed software
for autistic children and children with ADHD, rather than relying only on our
own ideas. Their experience is what turned our general idea into requirements
we can actually justify.

\textbf{Ahmed:} The team needs a mix of technical skills and an
understanding of the people we are building for. For my part, I need
to keep improving at turning a user flow into a clear interface and a
set of consistent, testable requirements. Rayan's backend work will
require reliable data modelling and access control, especially since
one adult account may have several learner profiles and practice
histories. Moustafa will need strong experience with browser drawing
input and responsive canvas behaviour, because the accuracy of
stylus events affects both evaluation and saved records.

Huzaifah will need a good verification and testing approach, including
ways to reproduce edge cases such as interrupted strokes, threshold
boundaries and failed saves. Shamil's role will benefit from strong
stakeholder communication and documentation skills so that decisions,
questions and feedback do not get lost between meetings. As a team,
we also need more domain knowledge about early visual-motor practice
and accessible interfaces. None of us should assume that something
which seems intuitive to us will necessarily work well for the children
or adults who would use Squigglr.

\textbf{Moustafa:} As the frontend lead, the main thing I need to learn is
how to handle stylus drawing in the browser properly. That means getting
accurate, smooth input from the stylus, handling things like palm rejection
and different screen sizes, and making sure what the child draws is exactly
what gets saved. I also need to learn how to actually build the
accessibility side I wrote in Sections 10 and 11, like checking contrast,
touch target sizes and text alternatives, instead of just specifying them.
For the team as a whole, I think the biggest skill is testing without
children. Since we can't run studies with them, we need to get good at
designing reviews with adults and prepared drawings that still tell us
something useful.

\item For each of the knowledge areas and skills identified in the previous
question, what are at least two approaches to acquiring the knowledge or
mastering the skill? Of the identified approaches, which will each team
member pursue, and why did they make this choice?

\textbf{Rayan:} The two approaches we chose for acquiring this domain
knowledge were interviews and questionnaires with domain specialists.
Moustafa and I pursued both. We interviewed three specialists on October 1,
2026, because a live conversation let us ask follow-up questions and explore
topics we had not expected, such as who should control a child's
progression. We then sent them a written questionnaire, answered between
October 2 and 4, 2026, to cover the topics the interview did not reach, such
as refinement in the creative space and letter activities. The questionnaire
also gave the specialists time to think through their answers. Using both
worked well together: the interview showed us what we did not know, and the
questionnaire filled in the gaps it left.

\textbf{Ahmed:} I see two useful approaches for each of these
areas: learning from established material and then testing what we
learn in something concrete. For my UI and requirements work, that
means reviewing HCI guidance and examples from similar applications,
then building prototypes and asking adults to walk through the flows.
The second approach matters because something that looks clear on
paper may still be confusing when somebody tries to use it.

For Rayan's data and access-control work, official documentation and
small integration prototypes can complement each other; I would
prioritize testing the multi-learner and saving flows early. For
Moustafa's stylus and canvas work, browser input documentation is a
starting point, but trying the same prepared strokes on different
touchscreen sizes will reveal problems that documentation cannot.
For Huzaifah, studying testing methods and building repeatable
synthetic test cases provide both the theory and evidence that our
requirements are met. For Shamil, reading relevant research and
following up with adult stakeholders through interviews or
questionnaires would help check our assumptions. These are the
approaches I would recommend we confirm as a team, because they let
us learn efficiently while still checking that what we build works
as intended.

\textbf{Moustafa:} For the stylus drawing, the two approaches are reading
the browser documentation on pointer and touch input, and building a small
drawing prototype early to test on real tablets with a stylus. I'll do
both, but lean on the prototype, because input problems like palm rejection
or lag only show up when you actually draw on the device. For
accessibility, I can either go through the WCAG 2.2 success criteria we
picked and learn the tools that check them, or look at how existing kids'
apps handle things like icons and touch targets. I'll start with WCAG and
the checking tools, since our requirements are written against those
criteria, so that's how they'll be tested. For testing without children, I
can learn from how other projects ran reviews with adults, or ask our
specialists how they evaluate apps when children can't take part. I'll go
with the specialists, because they've done exactly this before and offered
to help guide us.

\end{enumerate}
